has the subterm property on ground terms, we thus obtain
\ell\sigma\xRightarrow{\mathsf{tr}^{\prime}_{L}}s\theta
for some
\mathsf{tr}^{\prime}_{L}
where the positions in
\mathsf{tr}^{\prime}_{L}
are all different from the root position
\varepsilon
, i.e. they do not affect the root of
\ell
. Therefore, we deduce that there exists
u\xRightarrow{\mathsf{tr}_{L}}u[s\theta]_{p}
where
p
is a strict prefix of all positions of
\mathsf{tr}_{L}
.

The proof of
Theorem

5
and in particular the proof of
Item

M3
from
Definition

4
is completed by showing that when no more landscape transformation rule is applicable, the rewrite trace is a valley whose slopes are ordered by decreasing position. In such a case, as
f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})=_{E}t
,
\mathcal{M}=\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n},t\}
and
\mathrm{nf}_{T,E}(\mathcal{M})
holds, we can show that the ordered slopes is in fact only composed of a single rule, that is the one used to prove
Item

M3
.

VI
Optimisations

The procedure presented above has two main flaws: it may not terminate
and it may produce rewrite rules where variables of the right hand
side are not all included in the left hand side, the latter thus possibly violating the requirement from
Item

S3
of

Definition

2
. Although we will never be able to
guarantee termination (otherwise all equational theories would satisfy
the finite variant property, which is not the case), we suggest in
this section some optimisations that will help the procedure
terminate more often. Moreover, we will also propose an additional
optimisation that will ensure that all rewrite rules generated have
variables in their right hand side occurring in their left-hand side.

VI-A

Dealing with right-hand side variables

Recall that
>
is a
{E_{\downarrow}}
-strong reduction order and
{E_{\downarrow}}
has finite equivalence classes. Without restriction, the smallest ground term by
>
could be either a name or a constant. It cannot be any other term as
>
satisfies the subterm property on ground term, i.e. any strict subterm is strictly smaller than the term itself. To apply our optimisation, we will assume that the smallest ground term by
>
, denoted
\mathsf{a}_{\mathrm{min}}
, is a constant that does not occur in
{E_{\downarrow}}
. In such a way,
\mathsf{a}_{\mathrm{min}}
will be able to appear within our rewrite rules and not be affected by
{E_{\downarrow}}
. In practice, we can always augment
\mathcal{F}
with a new constant disjoint from
\mathcal{F}
, and define a new reduction from
>
where this constant is minimal.

The main idea of the rule comes from the following simple observation. Assume that
s=_{E}t
and
x\in\mathit{vars}(t)\setminus\mathit{vars}(s)
. Thus, for all terms
u_{1},u_{2}
, denoting
\sigma_{1}=\{x\mapsto u_{1}\}
and
\sigma_{2}=\{x\mapsto u_{2}\}
, we have
t\sigma_{1}=_{E}s=_{E}t\sigma_{2}
. In other words, the value of
x
does not really matter. Thus, when
s\rightarrow t
occurs in
\mathcal{R}
, we can replace
x
by the minimal term
\mathsf{a}_{\mathrm{min}}
. In order to achieve correctness, we also introduce the rewrite rule
t\rightarrow t\{x\mapsto\mathsf{a}_{\mathrm{min}}\}
. Therefore, we augment the set of normalisation rules with the following rule
Var
.

\begin{array}[]{l}(\textsc{Var})\\
\mathcal{R}\cup\{s\rightarrow t\}\rightsquigarrow_{{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}}}\mathcal{R}\cup\{s\rightarrow t\rho,t\rightarrow t\rho\}\\
\quad\hfill\text{if }\rho:V\rightarrow\{\mathsf{a}_{\mathrm{min}}\}\text{ and }V=(\mathit{vars}(t)\setminus\mathit{vars}(s))\neq\emptyset\end{array}

For example, this optimisation allows our prototype to generate a rewrite theory for the equational theory
\{g(x,y)=f(x,z);h(x)=a;f(a,z)=p(x,z)\}
.

VI-B

Checking the order of rules

So far, we assumed the existence of an
{E_{\downarrow}}
-strong reduction order
but it is only used in the proof and not in the algorithm
itself. However, if we have an effective way of testing whether
t>s
then we can remove any rule
s\rightarrow t
from
\mathcal{R}

such that
t>s
since they will never be used in a rewrite trace between two terms. Indeed,

u\xrightarrow[p,\sigma]{s\ \rightarrow\ t}v
and
u\xleftarrow[p,\sigma]{t\ \leftarrow\ s}v
requires that either
s\sigma=_{E_{\downarrow}}t\sigma
or
s\sigma>t\sigma
; and
t>s
implies
t\sigma>s\sigma
for all
\sigma
. In a similar fashion, as
>
satisfies the subterm property on ground terms,
s
cannot be a strict subterm of
t
as it would imply
t\sigma>s\sigma
. We can therefore augment the set of normalisation rules with the following rule
Ord
.

\begin{array}[]{l}(\textsc{Ord})\\
\mathcal{R}\cup\{s\rightarrow t\}\rightsquigarrow_{{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}}}\mathcal{R}\quad\text{if $t>s$ or $s\in\mathrm{st}^{s}(t)$}\end{array}

On the other hand, when a rule
s\rightarrow t
already satisfies
s>t
, we also have the opportunity to add it to
{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}}
as the only requirement on
{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}}
is to be
{E_{\downarrow}}
-terminating. When it occurs, we restart the algorithm by re-initialising
\mathcal{R}
to its initial value with the augmented
{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}}
. This optimisation allows the prototype to terminate on the equational theory
\{\mathrm{exp}(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{one})=g;\mathrm{exp}(\mathrm{exp}(\mathrm{g},x),y)=\mathrm{exp}(\mathrm{exp}(\mathrm{g},y),x)\}
.

VI-C

Subsumed rules modulo
{E_{\mathcal{A}}}

Theorem

5
generates a rewrite theory mimicking
E
, of the form
(>,\mathcal{R},{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}})
, where the
equational theory
{E_{\downarrow}}
used for normalisation is also used for the
computation of most general unifiers. As mentioned at the beginning of
the paper, we aim for rewrite theories of the form

(>,\mathcal{R},{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}},{E_{\mathcal{A}}})
. Of course, when
{E_{\downarrow}}\subseteq{E_{\mathcal{A}}}
, it is easy to see that if
(>,\mathcal{R},{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}})
mimics
E
then so does
(>,\allowbreak\mathcal{R},{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}},{E_{\mathcal{A}}})
. However, many of the rules in
\mathcal{R}
would become superfluous: typically, when two rules are equal modulo
{E_{\mathcal{A}}}
, only one of them is needed. We therefore consider a function that will remove these superfluous rules (
Algorithm

2
).

1

Function

cleanup(
\mathcal{R},{E_{\mathcal{A}}}
)

2

while

\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup\{s\rightarrow r,s^{\prime}\rightarrow r^{\prime}\}
and
\exists\alpha
s.t.
s\alpha=^{s}_{{E_{\mathcal{A}}}}s^{\prime}
and
r\alpha=_{{E_{\mathcal{A}}}}r^{\prime}

do

\mathcal{R}:=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup\{s\rightarrow r\}

3

return

\mathcal{R}

Algorithm 2

Removing superfluous rewrite rules

Correctness of this function is given below
(proof in
Section

-E
).

Lemma 4
.

If
(>,\mathcal{R},{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}},{E_{\mathcal{A}}})
is a rewrite theory mimicking an equational theory
E
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\textnormal{{cleanup(}}\textnormal{\emph{$\mathcal{R},{E_{\mathcal{A}}}$}}\textnormal{{)}}
then
(>,\mathcal{R}^{\prime},{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}},\allowbreak{E_{\mathcal{A}}})
is a rewrite theory mimicking
E
.

TABLE I:
Extract of equational theories handled by our prototype.

We developed a prototype implementing our semi-decision procedure in
OCaml and used Maude version 3.4
[
58
]
as backend. Our source code and examples are available in
[
42
]
. The prototype currently natively only support matching and unification modulo AC, meaning that given an input equational theory
E
, and an optional rewrite system
{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}}
, the prototype will execute
generate_rw_th(
E,{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}},AC
)
, returning a rewrite system
\mathcal{R}
.
In
Table

I
, we provide an extract of our experimental results on different equational theories, indicating the equations we consider, the computation time on a MacBook Pro M2 8-core with 24GB of memory, the number of rewrite rules generated by the algorithm (# rules), the size of
\mathcal{R}
(
|\mathcal{R}|
) and whether our prototype was able to determine that
\mathcal{R}
is convergent (Conv). Finally, we also indicate whether a rewrite system
{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}}
was initially provided to the prototype (
{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}}
). We explain below how our prototype check convergence.

In
Table

I
, we denote by AC
(+)
the equations for associativity and commutativity of the symbol
+
. In
[
27
]
, it was shown that adding to
\mathrm{XOR}
an homomorphic symbol
h
with the equation
h(x+y)=h(x)+h(y)
yields an equational theory that does not have the FVP modulo AC. However, we retrieve the FVP modulo AC when considering different operators
+_{\text{in}}
and
+_{\text{out}}
with the equation
\{h(x+_{\text{in}}y)=h(x)+_{\text{out}}h(y)\}
. In the case of
\mathrm{XOR}
for example, the equations
\mathrm{XOR}\cup H(+,+_{\text{out}})
ensure that
+_{\text{out}}
is also AC, nilpotent with a unit element but only over the outputs of the
h
function (not over all terms). The same technique applies to Abelian groups with an homomorphic symbol and (a)symmetric homomorphic encryption/decryption scheme.

We also consider more complex equations used in the literature. For instance, we consider a model of ElGamal asymmetric encryption used to model Exponentiation Mix-Nets in
[
57
]
. We also consider the Bilinear Pairing equations used in Tamarin, which is basically Diffie Hellman (DH) augmented with a bilinear map
\mathrm{em}
and a scalar multiplier
\cdot
. We relied on the generic definition where the function
\mathrm{em}
is not commutative, corresponding to the case where the groups used on the left and right arguments of
\mathrm{em}
may be different. Interestingly,
when adding the commutative property to
\mathrm{em}
, our algorithm seems
stuck
in the computation of an AC-unification in Maude. However, when considering
\mathrm{em}
to be both associative and commutative, our algorithm concludes and generates the same number of rewrite rules as the non-commutative case.
This can be explained by the fact that our prototype currently only implements natively AC unification and not commutative-only unification. Therefore the algorithm needs to take care of the commutative property which seems to lead to some very costly unification. In a future version of our prototype with commutative unification implemented, we expect the bilinear pairing with a commutative
\mathrm{em}
to conclude without problem.

Additional examples, including some toys examples, can be found in
[
42
]
.

We now discuss some additional observations on our procedure and its implementation.

VII-
1
Detecting convergent equational theories

Upon closer examination, our algorithm shares several similarities with the Knuth-Bendix algorithm used to show that an equational theory is convergent. The generation of
(r_{1}\rightarrow\ell_{1}\mathrel{\overset{p,{E_{\downarrow}}}{\rhd\mspace{-4mu}\lhd}}\ell_{2}\rightarrow r_{2})
and some of our normalisation rules intuitively correspond to the rules in the Knuth-Bendix algorithm. As such, it is hardly surprising that our algorithm also allows us to determine whether an equational theory can be represented by a rewrite system
\mathcal{R}
convergent modulo
{E_{\downarrow}}
. In particular, if our algorithms with optimisations returns the set
\mathcal{R}
then it suffices to check whether (almost) all rules in
\mathcal{R}
are ordered by
>
.
To be more specific, removing from
\mathcal{R}
the rules of the form
\ell\rightarrow\ell
added on
Algorithm

1
yields a rewrite system
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. It then suffices to check that
>
is compatible with
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
to show that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is
{E_{\downarrow}}
-convergent for
E
.

Termination is directly guaranteed. Confluence is given by
Theorem

1
. Indeed, denoting
T=(>,\allowbreak\mathcal{R},{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}})
, with a simple induction one can show that
\overline{t}\Downarrow_{T}t^{\prime}
implies that
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime},{E_{\downarrow}}}t^{\prime}
. Hence, the
{E_{\downarrow}}
-confluence of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is given by
Theorem

1
, since
t=_{E}s
implies that there exist
t^{\prime},s^{\prime}
such that
\overline{t}\Downarrow_{T}t^{\prime}
,
\overline{s}\Downarrow_{T}s^{\prime}
, and
t^{\prime}=_{E_{\downarrow}}s^{\prime}
. This leads to an interesting observation:

Lemma 5
.

If
E
has the finite variant property modulo
{E_{\downarrow}}
, then there exists a finite rewrite system
\mathcal{R}
that is
{E_{\downarrow}}
-confluent for
E
.

VII-
2
Existence of an AC-strong reduction order compatible with a rewrite system

Our procedure relies at minimum on the existence of an
{E_{\downarrow}}
-strong reduction order which, as previously mentioned, can be quite challenging to prove.
In our prototype, we rely on the AC-compatible and AC-total reduction order of
[
48
]
.
It is fully syntactic and RPO based, meaning that it is efficient and allows us to apply the optimisation described in
Section

VI-B
. Notice that for this order to be AC-strong, it additionally requires to be stable by renaming. Being RPO based, this can easily be achieved by encoding names into ground terms
\mathsf{N}=\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{sc}(\mathsf{a}),\allowbreak\mathsf{sc}(\mathsf{sc}(\mathsf{a})),\ldots\}
.

Although our experiments showed that using
>_{AC}
works well in practice, it can be enhanced by providing a rewrite system to the algorithm instead of letting the algorithm try to create one on its own. This is for example the case with the Abelian Group (
\mathcal{AG}
) equational theory. In
[
27
]
,
\mathcal{AG}
was shown to satisfy the FVP modulo AC using an AC-convergent rewrite system first proposed by Lankford
[
41
]
.
Denoting this rewrite system
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{AG}}
, we call our prototype with
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{AG}}
given as input. However, this is only correct if we can show the existence of an AC-strong reduction order compatible with
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{AG}}
. It is well known that from an
E
-terminating rewrite system, one can build an
E
-compatible reduction order, but obtaining totality is not always possible.
For example, consider the rewrite system
\mathcal{R}=\{f(x,x)\rightarrow k,k\rightarrow f(k_{1},k_{2})\}
with
k,k_{1},k_{2}
constants.
\mathcal{R}
is convergent but there exists no reduction order
\emptyset
-total and
\emptyset
-compatible with
\mathcal{R}
. Indeed, totality implies
f(k_{1},k_{2})>f(a,a)
for some minimal ground term
a
; and compatibility with
\mathcal{R}
entails that
f(a,a)>k>f(k_{1},k_{2})
, leading to a contradiction.

Nevertheless, we show the existence of an AC-strong reduction order compatible with
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{AG}}
by composing
>_{AC}
with the order that was used to show termination of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{AG}}

[
41
]
.
The composition of orders is given by the following result
(proof in
Section

-F
).

Lemma 6
.

Let
E
be an equational theory. Let
\equiv
be an equivalence relation on terms closed by application of contexts, substitutions and renaming, and such that
u=_{E}t
implies
u\equiv t
. Let
>_{1}
be an
E
-strong reduction order. Let
\mathcal{R}
be a set of rewrite rules and
>
be a reduction order stable by renaming such that:

•

if
s\equiv u>v\equiv t
then
s>t
(
\equiv
-compatible
);

•

for all ground terms
u,v
, either
u>v
or
v>u
or
u\equiv v
(
\equiv
-total
);

•

for all
(\ell\rightarrow r)\in\mathcal{R}
,
\ell>r
;

•

for all
a,b\in\mathcal{N}
,
a>b
implies
a>_{1}b
.

Then there exists an
E
-strong reduction order
>_{2}
compatible with
\mathcal{R}
.

Example 11
.

Consider the reduction order
>_{\#}
from
Example

2
and the order
>_{AC}
from
Example

3
. Define
\mathcal{R}=\{s\rightarrow t\mid s>_{\#}t\}
and
\equiv
the smallest equivalence relation such that
s\equiv t
implies either
s=_{AC}t
or there exist
a,b
ground terms and a term context
C[\_]
such that
s=C[a]
and
C[b]=t
and
\#(a)=\#(b)
. Applying
Lemma

6
with
>
being
>_{\#}
and
>_{1}
being
>_{AC}
, we deduce the existence of an
AC
-strong reduction order
>_{2}
compatible with
\mathcal{R}
. More precisely, the proof of
Lemma

6
defines
>_{2}
as the transitive closure of
>^{\prime}_{2}
where
s>^{\prime}_{2}t
iff:

•

either
s>_{\#}t

•

or
s\equiv t
and there exists
u,v
ground terms and a term context
C[\_]
such that
s=_{AC}C[u]
, and
t=_{AC}C[v]
, and
u>_{AC}v

In our prototype, we have also implemented the reduction order
>_{2}
. Note that increasing the number of reduction orders handled by our prototype also increases its chance to generate a rewrite theory mimicking the target equational theory. Indeed, changing the reduction order impacts the termination of the algorithm. It is however difficult to estimate beforehand which order is more suited to a given equational theory.

VII-
3
Limitations and future work

Although the optimised algorithm allows to transform more equational theories than
Algorithm

1
, both are still confined to the realm of theories having the FVP modulo
{E_{\downarrow}}
. The function
cleanup(
\mathcal{R},{E_{\mathcal{A}}}
)
takes
{E_{\mathcal{A}}}
into account by removing superfluous rules but does not change the fact that
\mathcal{R}
was built with
{E_{\downarrow}}
, i.e. the equational theory has the FVP modulo
{E_{\downarrow}}
. This is a limitation of our algorithm. Indeed, even if an equational theory
E
does not have the FVP modulo
E
(e.g.
\mathrm{XOR}
with homomorphic symbol),
E
trivially has the FVP modulo
E
(itself), and so we could expect other interesting equational theories that include
E
to have the FVP modulo
E
.
We conjecture that modifying the normalisation rule
Eq
and
Sub
to consider
=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}
instead of
=_{E_{\downarrow}}
should be a step in the right direction, possibly with the hypothesis that there exists a rewrite system
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}
that is
{E_{\downarrow}}
-convergent for
{E_{\mathcal{A}}}
. However, the proof we present in this paper cannot be as simply adapted. Indeed, by going from
{E_{\downarrow}}
to
{E_{\mathcal{A}}}
, we are losing very critical properties (
>
is not
{E_{\mathcal{A}}}
-compatible and
{E_{\mathcal{A}}}
does not have finite equivalence classes) for the termination of our transformations in the proof.
As future work, we plan to address these challenges.

Our prototype showed that for some examples relying on AC-unification, the computation time could go from several minutes up to more than one hour. Although our prototype was developed as a proof of concept, we used the tool Maude
[
58
]
to efficiently compute the most general unifiers modulo AC. However, most generated rules are duplicates of other generated rules. For example, applying our algorithm on ACN generates around 400k rules despite finally keeping only 14. We plan to identify new invariants in the proof of
Theorem

5
that would reduce the number of generated rules, hence increasing our algorithm’s efficiency.

Finally, the genericity of our prototype makes it an ideal candidate to be integrated to cryptographic verifiers such as Tamarin and ProVerif. We plan to first tackle its integration to ProVerif as the rewrite theory introduced in this paper is a generalisation of the framework used in ProVerif. Nevertheless, for both tools, it may require a major overhaul of the theory behind them. But this is, in our opinion, anyway the next logical step for automatic verifiers to handle more cryptographic primitives.

-A

Proofs of Section
II

Lemma 7
.

Let
E\subseteq E^{\prime}
be two equational theories. Let
>
be a
E
-strong reduction order. Let
k\in\mathcal{N}
be the smallest name by
>
. For all
t\in\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{N})
, for all renamings
\rho
preserving
>
, if

•

\mathit{names}(t)\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\rho)

•

k\in\mathit{img}(\rho)\Leftrightarrow k\in\mathit{dom}(\rho)

then
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t\rho)=_{E}{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t)\rho
.

Proof.

We first prove a small property on
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(\cdot)
: for all terms
u
, for all
a\in\mathit{names}({\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(u))\setminus\mathit{names}(u)
,
a=k
. Indeed, by definition,
u=_{E^{\prime}}{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(u)
. Thus, if
a\in\mathit{names}({\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(u))\setminus\mathit{names}(u)
, then we have that for all
b\in\mathcal{N}
,
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(u)\{a\mapsto b\}=_{E^{\prime}}u
. By contradiction, assume
k\neq a
. As
k
is the smallest name by
>
, we deduce that
a>k
. Since
>
is a reduction order, we deduce that
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(u)>{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(u)\{a\mapsto k\}
. This is in contradiction with the definition of
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(u)
considering that
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(u)=_{E^{\prime}}{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(u)\{a\mapsto k\}
.

We now proceed with the proof of the main result. Assume by contradiction that
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t\rho)\neq_{E}{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t)\rho
. Therefore,
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t)\rho>{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t\rho)
. By hypothesis,
\mathit{names}(t)\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\rho)
. If
k\not\in\mathit{dom}(\rho)
then we define
\rho^{\prime}=\rho\{k\mapsto k\}
else
\rho^{\prime}=\rho
. Hence, we deduce that
\rho^{\prime}
is a renaming preserving
>
such that
k\rho^{\prime}=k
and
k\in\mathit{dom}(\rho^{\prime})
. As such,
\rho^{-1}
is also a renaming preserving
>
.
From our small property, we know that
\mathit{names}({\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t))\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\rho^{\prime})
and
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t)\rho={\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t)\rho^{\prime}
. Similarly,
t\rho=t\rho^{\prime}
and
\mathit{names}({\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t\rho))\subseteq\mathit{img}(\rho^{\prime})
.

Therefore, we have
\mathit{names}({\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t\rho),{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t)\rho)\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\rho^{{}^{\prime}-1})
. As
>
is stable by renaming, we have
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t)>{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t\rho)\rho^{{}^{\prime}-1}
. Recall that
t=_{E^{\prime}}{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t)
, meaning that
t\rho=_{E^{\prime}}{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t)\rho
and so
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t\rho)=_{E^{\prime}}{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t)\rho
by definition of
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t\rho)
. This yields
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t\rho)\rho^{{}^{\prime}-1}=_{E^{\prime}}{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t)
, which is a contradiction with the definition of
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t)
and with
{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t)>{\min_{E^{\prime},>}}(t\rho)\rho^{{}^{\prime}-1}
.
∎

-B

Proof of Section
III

See
1

Proof.

Let
k
be the smallest name by
>
. By definition, there exists an injective substitution
\sigma:\mathit{vars}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
such that:

•

\forall a\in\mathit{img}(\sigma)
,
a\neq k
and
\forall b\in\mathit{names}(\mathcal{M})
,
a>b

•

\forall t\in\mathcal{M}
,
t\sigma=_{E_{\downarrow}}{\min_{E,>}}(t\sigma)
.

Before tackling the four properties, we start by proving the following property: for all
t\in\mathrm{st}(\mathcal{M})
,
t\sigma=_{E_{\downarrow}}{\min_{>,E}}(t\sigma)
. Assume by contradiction that there exist
t\in\mathcal{M}
and
p\in\mathcal{P}os(t)
such that
t_{|p}\sigma\neq_{E_{\downarrow}}{\min_{>,E}}(t_{|p}\sigma)
. By definition of
{\min_{>,E}}(t_{|p}\sigma)
,
t_{|p}\sigma=_{E}{\min_{>,E}}(t_{|p}\sigma)
. Since
>
is
{E_{\downarrow}}
-total, we deduce that
t_{|p}\sigma>{\min_{>,E}}(t_{|p}\sigma)
. Moreover,
>
is also an
{E_{\downarrow}}
-compatible reduction order, thus we deduce that
t\sigma>t\sigma[{\min_{>,E}}(t_{|p}\sigma)]_{p}
and
t\sigma=_{E}t\sigma[{\min_{>,E}}(t_{|p}\sigma)]_{p}
. It would contradict the fact that
t\sigma=_{E_{\downarrow}}{\min_{>,E}}(t\sigma)
.

Let us start with the proof of
Item

N1
.
Assume that
t
is reducible by
\rightarrow_{{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}}}
. Hence there exist
(\ell\rightarrow r)\in{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}}
, a substitution
\alpha
and
p\in\mathcal{P}os(t)
such that
t_{|p}=_{E_{\downarrow}}\ell\alpha
and
t\rightarrow_{{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}}}t[r\alpha]_{p}
. As
{E_{\downarrow}}
is regular and as
\mathit{vars}(r)\subseteq\mathit{vars}(\ell)
thanks to
Item

S3
, we deduce that
\mathit{vars}(r\alpha)\subseteq\mathit{vars}(t)
.

Moreover,
t_{|p}=_{E_{\downarrow}}\ell\alpha
implies
t_{|p}\sigma=_{E_{\downarrow}}\ell\alpha\sigma
, meaning that
t\sigma\rightarrow_{{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}}}t\sigma[r\alpha\sigma]_{p}
. By hypothesis, we deduce that
t\sigma=_{E}t\sigma[r\alpha\sigma]_{p}
. Since
t\sigma
is ground and
\mathit{vars}(r\alpha)\subseteq\mathit{vars}(t)
,
r\alpha\sigma
and
t\sigma[r\alpha\sigma]_{p}
are also ground. However, the ordering
>
is
{E_{\downarrow}}
-compatible with
{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}}
. Hence
t\sigma>t\sigma[r\alpha\sigma]_{p}
, which is a contradiction with
t\sigma=_{E_{\downarrow}}{\min_{E,>}}(t\sigma)
.

For the proof of
Item

N2
, our initial property gives us
s\sigma=_{E_{\downarrow}}{\min_{E,>}}(s\sigma)
and
t\sigma=_{E_{\downarrow}}{\min_{E,>}}(t\sigma)
. Moreover,
s=_{E}t
implies
s\sigma=_{E}t\sigma
. Therefore, by definition of
{\min_{E,>}}(\cdot)
,we have
{\min_{E,>}}(s\sigma)={\min_{E,>}}(t\sigma)
and then
s\sigma=_{E_{\downarrow}}t\sigma
. It entails
s=_{E_{\downarrow}}t
as
\sigma
is injective and
\mathit{names}(s,t)\cap\mathit{img}(\sigma)=\emptyset
.

For the proof of
Item

N3
, we start by showing that for all names
a
,
{\min_{E,>}}(a)=_{E_{\downarrow}}a
. Let us denote
t={\min_{E,>}}(a)
and assume that
t\neq_{E_{\downarrow}}a
, and then
t<a
. By definition,
t=_{E}a
. Assume first that
a\in\mathrm{st}(t)
. Since
>
is a reduction order,
a
cannot be a strict subterm of
t
(otherwise, if
t=C[a]
, we would have the infinite sequence of distinct terms
a>C[a]>C[C[a]]>C[C[C[a]]]>\ldots
, which is a contradiction with
>
being well-founded). Thus
a\in\mathrm{st}(t)
implies
t=a
, which is a contradiction with
t\neq_{E_{\downarrow}}a
. Assume now that
a\not\in\mathrm{st}(t)
. As equality modulo
E
is stable by replacement of names by arbitrary terms, we deduce that for all terms
t_{1},t_{2}
, we obtain
t_{1}=_{E}t=_{E}t_{2}
(by replacing
a
once by
t_{1}
and once by
t_{2}
). This contradicts the fact that
E
is a non-trivial equational theory.

We can now complete the proof of
Item

N3
as follows. If
x\not\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
, we can define
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma\{x\mapsto a\}
for some
a\in\mathcal{N}
such that
a
is different from
k
and greater than all names in
\mathit{img}(\sigma)
and in
\mathit{names}(\mathcal{M})
. Since
\mathcal{N}
is infinite, it is always possible to do so. If
x\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
, we define
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
. Hence,
\mathcal{M}\sigma=\mathcal{M}\sigma^{\prime}
and
x\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
. As we proved that for all names
a
,
{\min_{E,>}}(a)=_{E_{\downarrow}}a
, we obtain
{\min_{E,>}}(x\sigma^{\prime})=_{E_{\downarrow}}x\sigma^{\prime}
, which allows us to conclude.

Finally, let us focus on the proof of
Item

N4
. We first want to avoid the case where
\mathit{img}(\sigma)\cap\mathit{names}(t)\neq\emptyset
. We thus take a fresh renaming
\rho
preserving
>
, that renames the names in
\mathit{img}(\sigma)
. As
\mathcal{N}
is infinite, we can even take the new names to be all greater than the one in
\sigma
, i.e.
\mathit{dom}(\rho)=\mathit{img}(\sigma)
and for all
a\in\mathit{img}(\rho)
,
a\neq k
and for all
b\in\mathit{img}(\sigma)\cup\mathit{names}(t)
,
a>b
. Let us define
\rho^{\prime}=\rho\{a\mapsto a\mid a\in\mathit{names}(\mathcal{M},t)\}
. Hence, as
\rho
is preserving
>
and all names in
\mathit{img}(\rho)
are greater than the ones in
t
and
\mathcal{M}
, we deduce that
\rho^{\prime}
is also preserving
>
,
\mathit{names}(t\sigma,\mathcal{M}\sigma)\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\rho^{\prime})
and if
k\in\mathit{img}(\rho^{\prime})\cup\mathit{dom}(\rho^{\prime})
then
k\rho^{\prime}=k
.
Hence, by
Lemma

7
, we deduce that for all
v\in\mathcal{M}
,
v\sigma\rho^{\prime}=_{E_{\downarrow}}{\min_{E,>}}(v\sigma)\rho^{\prime}=_{E_{\downarrow}}{\min_{E,>}}(v\sigma\rho^{\prime})
. Finally, let us take the substitution
\alpha
such that for all
x\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
,
x\alpha=x\sigma\rho^{\prime}
and for all
x\in\mathit{vars}(t)\setminus\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
,
x\alpha
is a fresh name (we take it greater than the ones in
\rho^{\prime}
). We know that
t\alpha=_{E}{\min_{E,>}}(t\alpha)
. Hence, by taking
s
the term
{\min_{E,>}}(t\alpha)
where we replace all occurrences of names
a
by
a\alpha^{-1}
, we obtain that
s=_{E}t
, and
s\alpha={\min_{E,>}}(t\alpha)={\min_{E,>}}(s\alpha)
(since
{\min_{E,>}}({\min_{E,>}}(u))={\min_{E,>}}(u)
for all ground terms
u
). As we already showed that for all
v\in\mathcal{M}
,
v\alpha=_{E_{\downarrow}}{\min_{E,>}}(v\alpha)
, we conclude.
∎

-C

Proofs of Section
III

In this section, all lemmas consider an equational theory
E
and a rewrite theory
T=(>,\mathcal{R},{\mathcal{R}_{\downarrow}},{E_{\downarrow}},{E_{\mathcal{A}}})
mimicking
E
.

-C
1
Equality modulo
E

Lemma 8
.

Let
\sigma
be a substitution and
t,t^{\prime}
two terms. If
t^{\prime}=_{E}t\sigma
and
\mathrm{nf}_{T,E}(\{t^{\prime}\}\cup\{x\sigma\mid x\in\mathit{vars}(t)\})
then
\overline{t}\sigma\Downarrow_{T}t^{\prime\prime}
and
t^{\prime\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}t^{\prime}
for some
t^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
t
.

Case
t=x
:

By definition
\overline{x}=x
. Moreover, we have
x\sigma=_{E}t^{\prime}
. Since
\mathrm{nf}_{T,E}(\{x\sigma,t^{\prime}\})
, we deduce that
x\sigma=_{E_{\downarrow}}t^{\prime}
. By
Item

S1
of
Definition

2
, we know that
{E_{\downarrow}}\subseteq{E_{\mathcal{A}}}
hence the result holds with
t^{\prime\prime}=x\sigma
.

Case
t=f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
:

We have
t^{\prime}=_{E}f(t_{1}\sigma,\ldots,t_{n}\sigma)
. Let us denote
\mathcal{M}=\{t^{\prime}\}\cup\{x\sigma\in\mathit{vars}(t)\}
. By
Item

N4
of
Lemma

1
, there exists
t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{n}
such that
\mathrm{nf}_{T,E}(\mathcal{M}\cup\{t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{n}\})
. By
Item

M3
of
Definition

4
, there exist a substitution
\sigma^{\prime}
and
f(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\rightarrow r
in
\mathcal{R}
such that
t^{\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}r\sigma^{\prime}
and for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
\ell_{i}\sigma^{\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}t^{\prime}_{i}
. By applying our inductive hypothesis on
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
, we deduce that there exist
t^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime\prime}_{n}
such that for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
\overline{t_{i}}\sigma\Downarrow_{T}t^{\prime\prime}_{i}
and
t^{\prime\prime}_{i}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}t^{\prime}_{i}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}\ell_{i}\sigma^{\prime}
. By
Definition

6
, we deduce that
\overline{f}(\overline{t_{1}},\ldots,\overline{t_{n}})\Downarrow_{T}r\sigma^{\prime}
. Since we already showed that
r\sigma^{\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}t^{\prime}
, we conclude.
∎

Lemma 9
.

Let
t
be a TE-term. If
t\Downarrow_{T}s
then
\underline{t}=_{E}s
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
t
.

Case
t\in\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{X})
:

In such a case
\underline{t}=t\Downarrow_{T}t
and so the result holds.

Case
t=\overline{f}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
:

By definition,
t\Downarrow_{T}s
implies that there exists
f(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\rightarrow r\in\mathcal{R}
and a substitution
\sigma
such that
s=r\sigma
and for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
t_{i}\Downarrow_{T}s_{i}
and
s_{i}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}\ell_{i}\sigma
. By
Item

M1
, we know that
{E_{\mathcal{A}}}\subseteq E
. Hence
s_{i}=_{E}\ell_{i}\sigma
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Moreover, by
Item

M2
of
Definition

4
, we know that
f(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})=_{E}r
. Thus
f(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})=_{E}f(\ell_{1}\sigma,\ldots,\ell_{n}\sigma)=_{E}r\sigma
. Applying our inductive hypothesis on
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
gives us
\underline{t_{i}}=_{E}s_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. This allows us to deduce that
\underline{t}=f(\underline{t_{1}},\ldots,\underline{t_{n}})=_{E}r\sigma=s
.
∎

See
1

Proof.

Assume that
t=_{E}s
. By
Item

N4
of
Lemma

1
, we know that there exists
u
such that
u=_{E}t
and
\mathrm{nf}_{T,E}(\{u\})
(by taking
\mathcal{M}=\emptyset
). Hence, by
Item

N3
of
Lemma

1
, we deduce that
\mathrm{nf}_{T,E}(\{u\}\cup\mathit{vars}(t,s))
. Applying
Lemma

8
, we obtain that there exist
t^{\prime},s^{\prime}
such that
\overline{t}\Downarrow_{T}t^{\prime}
and
\overline{s}\Downarrow_{T}s^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}u=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}s^{\prime}
.

Assume now that there exist
t^{\prime},s^{\prime}
such that
\overline{t}\Downarrow_{T}t^{\prime}
,
\overline{s}\Downarrow_{T}s^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}s^{\prime}
. We know from
Item

M1
that
{E_{\mathcal{A}}}\subseteq E
thus
t^{\prime}=_{E}s^{\prime}
. Moreover, by
Lemma

9
,
t=_{E}t^{\prime}
and
s=_{E}s^{\prime}
.
∎

-C
2
Properties on open evaluation

For the next proofs, we will define the size of an evaluation term
t
, denoted
|t|_{TE}
, as the number of function symbols from
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
in
t
. We also naturally extend the definition of close evaluation to sequence of ET-term, as follows:
[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]\Downarrow_{T}[s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}]
if
t_{i}\Downarrow_{T}s_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Given two sequences of terms
L,L^{\prime}
, we also write
L=_{E}L^{\prime}
when
L=[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
,
L^{\prime}=[s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}]
and for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
t_{i}=_{E}s_{i}
.

Lemma 10
.

Let
L
be a sequence of TE-terms. If
L\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}(L_{t},\sigma)
then
\mathit{dom}(\sigma)\cap\mathit{vars}(L_{t})=\emptyset
.

Proof.

We prove the result by induction on
\{\!\!\{|t|_{TE}\mid t\in L\}\!\!\}
.

Case
L=[]
:

In such a case,
L_{t}=[]
and
\sigma=\emptyset
hence the result trivially holds.

Case
L=[t]
with
t\in T(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{N})
:

In such a case,
L_{t}=L
and
\sigma=\emptyset
. Hence the result holds.

Case
L=[\overline{f}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})]
:

Let us denote
L^{\prime}=[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
. We know that there exist
f(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\rightarrow r\in\mathcal{R}
and
L^{\prime}_{t}
,
\sigma^{\prime},\sigma_{u}
such that, denoting
L_{\ell}=[\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}]
,
L^{\prime}\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}(L^{\prime}_{t},\sigma^{\prime})
, and
\sigma_{u}\in\mathrm{mgu}_{{E_{\mathcal{A}}}}(L^{\prime}_{t},L_{\ell})
, and
L_{t}=[r\sigma_{u}]
and
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\sigma_{u}
.

Let
x\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma)\cap\mathit{vars}(L_{t})
. Hence either
x\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma_{u})
or
x\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma^{\prime})
. In the former case, since
\sigma_{u}
is acyclic then
x\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma_{u})
implies that
x\not\in\mathit{vars}(r\sigma_{u})
which is in contradiction with
x\in\mathit{vars}(L_{t})
. Hence
x\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma^{\prime})
. By our inductive hypothesis, it implies that
x\not\in\mathit{vars}(L^{\prime}_{t})
. Since the variables of
f(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\rightarrow r\in\mathcal{R}
can always be renaming and since the variables of a most general unifier
\sigma_{u}\in\mathrm{mgu}_{{E_{\mathcal{A}}}}(L^{\prime}_{t},L_{\ell})
can also be renamed, we have that
x\not\in\mathit{vars}(\sigma_{u})
and
x\not\in L_{\ell}
. Hence
x\not\in\mathit{vars}(L^{\prime}_{t}\sigma_{u})
and
x\not\in L_{\ell}\sigma_{u}
. But
\mathit{vars}(r\sigma_{u})\subseteq\mathit{vars}(L_{\ell}\sigma_{u})
. Hence
x\not\in\mathit{vars}(L_{t})
which is a contradiction with
x\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma)\cap\mathit{vars}(L_{t})
. This conclude the proof that
\mathit{dom}(\sigma)\cap\mathit{vars}(L_{t})=\emptyset
.
∎

Lemma 11
.

For all sequences of TE-terms
L
, if
L\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}(L_{t},\sigma)
then
\underline{L}\sigma=_{E}L_{t}
.

Proof.

We prove the result by induction on
\{\!\!\{|t|_{TE}\mid t\in L\}\!\!\}
.

Case
L=[]
:

In such a case,
L_{t}=[]
hence the result trivially holds.

Case
L=[t]
with
t\in T(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{N})
:

In such a case,
\underline{L_{t}}=L_{t}=L
and
\sigma=\emptyset
. Hence the result holds.

Case
L=[\overline{f}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})]
:

Let us denote
L^{\prime}=[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
. We know that there exist
f(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\rightarrow r\in\mathcal{R}
and
L^{\prime}_{t}
,
\sigma^{\prime},\sigma_{u}
such that, denoting
L_{\ell}=[\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}]
,
L^{\prime}\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}(L^{\prime}_{t},\sigma^{\prime})
, and
\sigma_{u}\in\mathrm{mgu}_{{E_{\mathcal{A}}}}(L^{\prime}_{t},L_{\ell})
, and
L_{t}=[r\sigma_{u}]
and
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\sigma_{u}
. By inductive hypothesis on
L^{\prime}
, we know that
\underline{L^{\prime}}\sigma^{\prime}=_{E}L^{\prime}_{t}
. Hence,
\underline{L}\sigma^{\prime}\sigma_{u}=_{E}L^{\prime}_{t}\sigma_{u}=_{E}L_{\ell}\sigma_{u}
as
{E_{\mathcal{A}}}\subseteq E
by
Item

M1
. Note that the variables of
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n},r
are fresh, i.e.
\mathit{vars}(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n},r)\cap\mathit{vars}(\sigma)=\emptyset
. Hence
L_{\ell}\sigma\sigma_{u}=L_{\ell}\sigma_{u}
and
r\sigma\sigma_{u}=r\sigma_{u}
. This allow us to deduce that
f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\sigma=_{E}f(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\sigma
. By
Item

M2
,
f(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})=_{E}r
and so
f(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\sigma=r\sigma
which allows us to conclude.

Case
L=t\cdot L^{\prime}
:

In such a case, we have
[t]\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}([s],\sigma^{\prime})
and
L^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}(L^{\prime}_{t},\sigma^{\prime\prime})
with
L_{t}=s\sigma^{\prime\prime}\cdot L^{\prime}_{t}
and
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime\prime}
. Note that
\underline{L^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}=\underline{L^{\prime}}\sigma^{\prime}
. By inductive hypothesis on both
[t]
and
L^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}
, we deduce that
\underline{t}\sigma^{\prime}=_{E}s
and
\underline{L^{\prime}}\sigma^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime\prime}=_{E}L^{\prime}_{t}
. Hence
(\underline{t}\cdot\underline{L^{\prime}})=_{E}s\sigma^{\prime\prime}\cdot L^{\prime}_{t}
and so
\underline{L}\sigma=_{E}L_{t}
.
∎

Lemma 12
.

Let
t
be a TE-term and two substitutions
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
such that
t\sigma_{1}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}t\sigma_{2}
. For all terms
s_{1}
, if
t\sigma_{1}\Downarrow_{T}s_{1}
then there exists a term
s_{2}
such that
t\sigma_{2}\Downarrow_{T}s_{2}
and
s_{1}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}s_{2}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
t
.

Case
t\in\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{N})
:

In that case,
t\sigma_{1}\Downarrow_{T}t\sigma_{1}
and
t\sigma_{2}\Downarrow_{T}t\sigma_{2}
. Thus the result holds.

Case
t=\overline{f}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
:

In such a case, by
Definition

6
, there exists
f(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\rightarrow r\in\mathcal{R}
and
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
terms and a substitutions
\sigma
such that for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
t_{i}\sigma_{1}\Downarrow_{T}s_{i}
and
s_{i}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}\ell_{i}\sigma
. By inductive hypothesis on
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
, there exist terms
s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}
such that for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
t_{i}\sigma_{2}\Downarrow_{T}s^{\prime}_{i}
and
s_{i}={E_{\mathcal{A}}}s^{\prime}_{i}
and so
s^{\prime}_{i}={E_{\mathcal{A}}}\ell_{i}\sigma
. This leads to
t\sigma_{2}\Downarrow_{T}r\sigma
.
∎

Lemma 13
.

Let
L
be a sequence of TE-terms. For all substitutions
\sigma
, for all sets of variables
\mathcal{V}
, if
L\sigma\Downarrow_{T}L_{s}
and
\mathit{dom}(\sigma)\cup\mathit{vars}(L,L_{s})\subseteq\mathcal{V}
then there exist a sequence of term
L_{t}
, two substitutions
\alpha,\beta
such that
L\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}(L_{t},\alpha)
, and
L_{t}\beta=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}L_{s}
and
\sigma=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}(\alpha\beta)_{|\mathit{dom}(\sigma)}
and
\mathit{dom}(\alpha\beta)\cap\mathcal{V}=\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

We prove this result by induction on
\{\!\!\{|t|_{TE}\mid t\in L\}\!\!\}
.

Case
L=[]
:

In such case,
[]\Downarrow_{T}[]
and
[]\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}([],\emptyset)
. So the result holds directly with
L_{t}=L
,
\alpha=\emptyset
and
\beta=\sigma
.

Case
L=[t]
with
t\in\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{N})
:

In such a case,
[t\sigma]\Downarrow_{T}[t\sigma]
and
[t]\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}([t],\emptyset)
. Once again the result directly holds with
L_{t}=L
,
\alpha=\emptyset
and
\beta=\sigma
.

Case
L=[\overline{f}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})]
:

Let us denote
t=\overline{f}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
. By definition,
L\sigma\Downarrow_{T}L_{s}
implies that there exist a term
s
such that
L_{s}=[s]
and
t\sigma\Downarrow_{T}s
. Hence there exist
f(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\rightarrow r\in\mathcal{R}
and a substitution
\theta
and terms
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
such that
r\theta=s
and for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
\ell_{i}\theta=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}s_{i}
and
t_{i}\sigma\Downarrow_{T}s_{i}
.
Let
L^{\prime}=[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
and
L^{\prime}_{s}=[s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}]
. By inductive hypothesis on
L^{\prime}
with
\sigma
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathcal{V}\cup\mathit{vars}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
, there exists a sequence of terms
L^{\prime}_{t}
and two substitutions
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}
such that
L^{\prime}\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}(L^{\prime}_{t},\alpha^{\prime})
, and
L^{\prime}_{t}\beta^{\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}L^{\prime}_{s}
,
\sigma=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}(\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime})_{|\mathit{dom}(\sigma)}
and
\mathit{dom}(\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
.
Since the variables of
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{m},r
can always be renamed, we can w.l.o.g. assume that
\mathit{dom}(\theta)\cap\mathit{vars}(\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime})=\emptyset
and
\mathit{dom}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\emptyset
.

Since
L^{\prime}_{t}\beta^{\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}L^{\prime}_{s}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}[\ell_{1}\theta,\ldots,\ell_{n}\theta]
, we can define
L_{\ell}=[\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}]
and
\gamma=\beta^{\prime}\theta
to have
L^{\prime}_{t}\beta^{\prime}=L^{\prime}_{t}\gamma
and
L_{\ell}\theta=L_{\ell}\gamma
and
r\gamma=r\theta
. Hence
\gamma
is a
{E_{\mathcal{A}}}
-unifier of
L^{\prime}_{t}
and
L_{\ell}
. Hence, there exist
\sigma_{u}\in\mathrm{mgu}_{{E_{\mathcal{A}}}}(L^{\prime}_{t},L_{\ell})
and
\theta_{u}
such that for all
x\in\mathit{vars}(L^{\prime}_{t},L_{\ell})
,
x\gamma=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}x\sigma_{u}\theta_{u}
. Note that using renaming of variables in
\sigma_{u}
introduced by the computation of most general unifier, we can always have
\gamma=(\sigma_{u}\theta_{u})_{\mathit{dom}(\gamma)}
and the variables of
\mathit{dom}(\sigma_{u}\theta_{u})\setminus\mathit{dom}(\gamma)
are all fresh.
Hence by
Definition

7
,
L\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}([r\sigma_{u}],\alpha^{\prime}\sigma_{u})
. Let us define
\beta=\theta_{u}
and
L_{t}=[r\sigma_{u}]
. By
Item

S3
,
\mathit{vars}(r)\subseteq\mathit{vars}(L_{\ell})
, hence
r\sigma_{u}\theta_{u}=r\gamma
. It implies that
L_{t}\beta=[r\sigma_{u}\theta_{u}]=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}[r\gamma]=[r\theta]=[s]=L_{s}
. Moreover, for all
x\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
,
x\sigma=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}x\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}
.
As
\mathit{dom}(\theta)\cap\mathit{vars}(\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime})=\emptyset
,
x\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}=x\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\theta=x\alpha^{\prime}\gamma=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}x\alpha^{\prime}\sigma_{u}\theta_{u}
.

Hence, defining
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}\sigma_{u}
, we obtain
x\sigma=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}x\alpha\beta
. Take
x\in\mathit{dom}(\alpha\beta)\cap\mathcal{V}
. Note that
\mathit{dom}(\alpha\beta)=\mathit{dom}(\alpha^{\prime}\sigma_{u}\theta_{u})
. But
\mathit{dom}(\sigma_{u}\theta_{u})
is
\mathit{dom}(\gamma)
with some fresh variables introduced in the computation of the most general unifier. However,
\mathit{dom}(\gamma)=\mathit{dom}(\beta^{\prime})\cup\mathit{dom}(\theta)
and we already know that
\mathit{dom}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\emptyset
. Since
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
, we deduce that
\mathit{dom}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{V}=\emptyset
. Therefore,
\mathit{dom}(\alpha\beta)\cap\mathcal{V}=(\mathit{dom}(\alpha^{\prime})\cup\mathit{dom}(\beta^{\prime}))\cap\mathcal{V}=\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
.

Case
L=t\cdot L^{\prime}
:

By definition
L\sigma\Downarrow_{T}L_{s}
implies that
t\sigma\Downarrow_{T}s
and
L^{\prime}\sigma\Downarrow_{T}L^{\prime}_{s}
and
L_{s}=s\cdot L^{\prime}_{s}
for some term
s
and sequence of terms
L^{\prime}_{s}
. By inductive hypothesis, there exists a term
t^{\prime}
and two substitutions
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}
such that
[t]\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}([t^{\prime}],\alpha^{\prime})
and
t^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}s
and
\sigma=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}(\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime})_{|\mathit{dom}(\sigma)}
and
\mathit{dom}(\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{V}=\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
.

Since
\mathit{vars}(L)\subseteq\mathcal{V}
, we deduce that
L^{\prime}\sigma=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}L^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}
. By
Lemma

12
, we deduce that there exists a sequence of terms
L^{\prime\prime}_{s}
such that
L^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\Downarrow_{T}L^{\prime\prime}_{s}
and
L^{\prime\prime}_{s}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}L^{\prime}_{s}
. By applying our inductive hypothesis on
(L^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime})
and
\beta^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathcal{V}\cup\mathit{vars}(L^{\prime\prime}_{s})\cup\mathit{dom}(\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime})\cup\mathit{vars}(t^{\prime})
, we obtain that there exists a sequence of terms
L^{\prime\prime}_{t}
, two substitutions
\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}
such that
L^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}(L^{\prime\prime}_{t},\alpha^{\prime\prime})
and
L^{\prime\prime}_{t}\beta^{\prime\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}L^{\prime\prime}_{s}
and
\beta^{\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}(\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime})_{|\mathit{dom}(\beta^{\prime})}
and
\mathit{dom}(\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime})\cap\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathit{dom}(\beta^{\prime})
.

By
Definition

7
,
L\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}(t^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}\cdot L^{\prime\prime}_{t},\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime})
. Let us denote
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}
and
\beta=\beta^{\prime\prime}
and
L_{t}=t^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}\cdot L^{\prime\prime}_{t}
. Note that
\mathit{vars}(t^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. Hence
t^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}t^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}s
. Moreover,
L^{\prime\prime}_{t}\beta^{\prime\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}L^{\prime\prime}_{s}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}L^{\prime}_{s}
. Hence,
L_{t}\beta=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}L_{s}
. Let us show
\sigma=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}(\alpha\beta)_{\mathit{dom}(\sigma)}
. Let
x\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
. We know that
x\sigma=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}x\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}
. As
\beta^{\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}(\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime})_{|\mathit{dom}(\beta^{\prime})}
, we obtain
x\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}x\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}=x\alpha\beta
. It also implies that
\mathit{dom}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\alpha\beta)
. As
\mathit{dom}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathcal{V}
, we have
\mathit{dom}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\alpha\beta)\cap\mathcal{V}
. Take
x\in\mathit{dom}(\alpha\beta)\cap\mathcal{V}
. Hence
x\in\mathit{dom}(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime})=\mathit{dom}(\alpha^{\prime})\cup\mathit{dom}(\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime})
. If
x\in\mathit{dom}(\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime})
then, since
\mathit{dom}(\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime})\cap\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathit{dom}(\beta^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
, we deduce that
x\in\mathit{dom}(\beta^{\prime})
. Therefore
x\in\mathit{dom}(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime})
implies
x\in\mathit{dom}(\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime})
. As we proved that
\mathit{dom}(\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{V}=\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
, we conclude that
x\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
and so
\mathit{dom}(\alpha\beta)\cap\mathcal{V}=\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
.
∎

Lemma 14
.

For all sequences of terms
L,L_{s}
, for all substitutions
\sigma
, if
L\sigma=_{E}L_{s}
and
\mathrm{nf}_{T,E}(L_{s})
then there exist
\overline{L}\Downarrow^{\prime}_{T}(L_{t},\alpha)
and a substitution
\beta
such that
L_{t}\beta=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}L_{s}
and
\sigma=_{E}(\alpha\beta)_{\mathit{dom}(\sigma)}
and
\mathit{vars}(L)\cap\mathit{dom}(\alpha\beta)\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

By

of

, there exists a substitution
\sigma^{\prime}
such that
\mathrm{nf}_{T,E}(L_{s}\cup\mathit{img}(\sigma^{\prime}))
and
\sigma=_{E}\sigma^{\prime}
. Moreover, by

of
Lemma

, we deduce that
\mathrm{nf}_{T,E}(L_{s}\cup\mathit{img}(\sigma^{\prime})\cup\{x\sigma^{\prime}\mid x\in\mathit{vars}(L)\})
. By
Lemma

8
,
\overline{L}\sigma^{\prime}\Downarrow_{T}L^{\prime}_{s}
and
L^{\prime}_{s}=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}L_{s}
for some
L^{\prime}_{s}
.
Take
\mathcal{V}=\mathit{dom}(\sigma^{\prime})\cup\mathit{vars}(L,L^{\prime}_{s})
. By
Lemma

, there exists a sequence of terms

, two substitutions

∎

Proof.

For all

To prove

. As for all

.

It remains to prove

. Take

with

and

. Hence, by

, there exists

,

,

. Take

. Since

.

If we denote

p,p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
are positions of

. Moreover, we can show that

and for all

. Indeed, assume by contradiction that this is not the case, w.l.o.g.

{\min_{E,>}}(u_{|p}\alpha\sigma)<w_{|p}
(since

). However,

-compatible which implies that
w[{\min_{E,>}}(u_{|p}\alpha\sigma)]_{p}<w
. This leads to a contradiction with

.

u^{\prime}_{|p}\gamma=_{E_{\mathcal{A}}}{\min_{E,>}}(u_{|p}\alpha\sigma)
and for all

,

. Recall that

.

When

.

We now look at the case

. Recall that we already proved that

. By definition of

, we know that either

. As

. Therefore,

. Once again, as

, we conclude by taking the substitution

and the rule

in
